|c_{\ell j,\bm{\kappa}}|\leq A^{\chi}_{\ell j}{\rho_{1}^{\chi|\kappa_{1}|}}{\rho_{2}^{\chi|\kappa_{2}|}}{\rho_{3}^{\chi\kappa_{3}}}
, for some finite
\rho_{1}^{\chi},\rho_{2}^{\chi},\rho_{3}^{\chi}\in(0,1)
independent of
\ell
,
j
, and
\bm{\kappa}
, and some finite
A^{\chi}_{\ell j}>0
independent of
\bm{\kappa}
and such that

\sum_{j=1}^{q}A_{\ell j}^{\chi}\leq A^{\chi}
, for some finite
A^{\chi}>0
independent of
\ell
;

(ii)

|\beta_{\ell j,\bm{\kappa}}|\leq A^{\xi}_{\ell j}{\rho_{1}^{\xi|\kappa_{1}|}}{\rho_{2}^{\xi|\kappa_{2}|}}{\rho_{3}^{\xi\kappa_{3}}}
, for some finite
\rho_{1}^{\xi},\rho_{2}^{\xi},\rho_{3}^{\xi}\in(0,1)
independent of
\ell
,
j
, and
\bm{\kappa}
, and some finite
A^{\xi}_{\ell j}>0
independent of
\bm{\kappa}
and such that

\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}A^{\xi}_{\ell j}\leq A^{\xi}
and
\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}A^{\xi}_{\ell j}\leq A^{\xi}
, for some finite
A^{\xi}>0
independent of
\ell
and
j
.

This assumption implies square-summability of the coefficients of the filters, which for the common component is a sufficient condition for (ii) in Definition
4.5
to hold, see Remark
4.1
. This assumption has two other important implications. First, part (ii) implies that the largest spatio-temporal dynamic eigenvalue of
\bm{\xi}_{n}
satisfies (see Proposition
E.1
in Appendix
E
)

\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}}\lim_{n\to\infty}\lambda_{n1}^{\xi}(\bm{\theta})\leq C,

(21)

for some finite
C>0
. Hence, according to (i) in Theorem
4.1
,
\bm{\xi}_{n}
is effectively an idiosyncratic component. Second, in part (i) we do not require summability of the coefficients along the rows, so that the spatio-temporal dynamic eigenvalues of
\bm{\chi}_{n}
can be diverging with
n
. Divergence of those eigenvalues is made formal by means of the following assumption which strengthens (ii) in Theorem
4.1
:

Assumption 6.3
.

For all
j=1,\ldots,q-1
there exist continuous functions

\bm{\theta}\mapsto\widetilde{\omega}_{j}(\bm{\theta})
and
\bm{\theta}\mapsto\undertilde{\omega}_{j}(\bm{\theta})

such that for all
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}

0<\undertilde{\omega}_{j+1}(\bm{\theta})\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\lambda_{n,j+1}^{\chi}(\bm{\theta})}{n}\leq\widetilde{\omega}_{j+1}(\bm{\theta})<\undertilde{\omega}_{j}(\bm{\theta})\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\lambda_{nj}^{\chi}(\bm{\theta})}{n}\leq\widetilde{\omega}_{j}(\bm{\theta})<\infty.

The requirements of distinct and linearly diverging eigenvalues are standard in the factor model literature. While the former requirement is merely technical, the latter implies that here we are dealing only with factors which are pervasive for the whole cross-section, which in turn implies that the ordering of the cross-sectional units is irrelevant for estimation.
Both requirements could, in principle be relaxed. For example, the case of local, or group specific dynamic factors, could be considered along the lines of what done by
Hallin and
Liška 2011
in the purely time series case. We do not make any distributional assumption but we require only the following moment conditions

Assumption 6.4
.

For all
j=1,\ldots,q
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

\max\left\{{\rm E}\left(|v_{h\bm{\varsigma}}|^{p}\right),{\rm E}\left(|\varepsilon_{j\bm{\varsigma}}|^{p}\right)\right\}\leq\bar{A}
,
for some
p>4
and
\bar{A}>0
independent of
j
and
\ell
.

Two technical assumptions are also required.
First, we characterize the kernel functions and bandwidths needed to estimate the spectral density matrix and the truncation levels in (
19
) by the following

Assumption 6.5
.

(i)

For any
l=1,2,3
, the kernel functions
K_{l}:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
are symmetric and bounded, and such that

(a)

K_{l}(0)=1
;

(b)

for some
\vartheta_{l}>0
,
|K_{l}(u)-1|=O(|u|^{\vartheta_{l}})
as
u\rightarrow 0
;

(c)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{l}^{2}(u){\rm d}u<\infty
;

(d)

\sum_{h_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\sup_{|h_{1}-h_{2}|\leq 1}|K_{l}(h_{1}u)-K_{l}(h_{2}u)|=O(1)
as
u\rightarrow 0
.

(ii)

The bandwidths are such that

c_{1}S_{1}^{b_{1}}<B_{S_{1}}<c_{2}S_{1}^{b_{2}}
,
c_{1}^{*}S_{2}^{b_{1}^{*}}<B_{S_{2}}<c_{2}^{*}S_{2}^{b_{2}^{*}}
, and
c_{1}^{**}T^{b_{1}^{**}}<B_{T}<c_{2}^{**}T^{b_{2}^{**}}
, for some

c_{1},c_{2},c_{1}^{*},c_{2}^{*},c_{1}^{**},c_{2}^{**}>0
and
0<b_{1}<b_{2}<1
,
0<b_{1}^{*}<b_{2}^{*}<1
,
0<b_{1}^{**}<b_{2}^{**}<1
.

(iii)

d_{1}S_{1}^{p_{1}}<M_{S_{1}}<d_{2}S_{1}^{p_{2}}
,

d_{1}^{*}S_{2}^{p_{1}^{*}}<M_{S_{2}}<d_{2}^{*}S_{2}^{p_{2}^{*}}
, and

d_{1}^{**}T^{p_{1}^{**}}<M_{T}<d_{2}^{**}T^{p_{2}^{**}}
, for some

d_{1},d_{2},d_{1}^{*},d_{2}^{*},d_{1}^{**},d_{2}^{**}>0
and
0<p_{1}<p_{2}<1
,
0<p_{1}^{*}<p_{2}^{*}<1
,
0<p_{1}^{**}<p_{2}^{**}<1
.

Part (i) and (ii) are standard. Part (iii) controls the truncation of the linear filter defined in (
18
) and (
19
).

Second, we assume that the effect of the linear spatio-temporal filters
{\underline{{\bm{K}}}}^{\chi}_{n\ell}(L)
, as defined in (
16
), decreases geometrically.

Assumption 6.6
.

For any
\ell=1,\ldots,n
,
let
{\underline{{\bm{K}}}}^{\chi}_{n\ell}(L)=\sum_{(\kappa_{1}\ \kappa_{2}\ \kappa_{3})^{\top}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}{{{\bm{K}}}}^{\chi}_{n\ell,(\kappa_{1}\ \kappa_{2}\ \kappa_{3})}

L_{1}^{\kappa_{1}}L_{2}^{\kappa_{2}}L_{3}^{\kappa_{3}}
,
then,

\|{{{\bm{K}}}}^{\chi}_{n\ell,(\kappa_{1}\ \kappa_{2}\ \kappa_{3})}\|\leq C_{0}(1+\varepsilon_{1})^{-|\kappa_{1}|}(1+\varepsilon_{2})^{-|\kappa_{2}|}(1+\varepsilon_{3})^{-|\kappa_{3}|}\|{{{\bm{K}}}}^{\chi}_{n\ell,(0\ 0\ 0)}\|,

for some finite
C_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3}>0
independent of
\ell
.

6.3
Asymptotic results

To study the asymptotic properties of the estimated spectral density matrix, we generalize to the case of spatio-temporal rf the approaches by
Wu and Zaffaroni 2018
and
Zhang and Wu 2021
for time series and by
Deb, Pourahmadi and Wu 2017
for purely spatial models, which in turn are all are based on the notion of functional dependence originally proposed by
Wu 2005
in a univariate time series context.
The resulting estimation theory is available in Appendix
D
and represents a novel contribution to the literature on the inference for spatio-temporal rf.

Letting
\widehat{\sigma}_{ij}^{x}(\bm{\theta})
be the
(i,j)
-th entry of the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}^{x}(\bm{\theta})
, defined in (
17
), we prove the following

Theorem 6.1
.

Let Assumptions
3.1
,
3.2
,
6.1
,
6.2
,
6.4
, and
6.5
hold. Then, there exists a finite
C>0
independent of
n,S_{1},S_{2}
and
T
, such that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}}{\rm E}\left|\widehat{\sigma}_{ij}^{x}(\bm{\theta})-\sigma_{ij}^{x}(\bm{\theta})\right|^{2}=C\max\left\{\frac{(\log B_{S_{1}}\log B_{S_{2}}\log B_{T})^{2}B_{S_{1}}B_{S_{2}}B_{T}}{S_{1}S_{2}T},\frac{1}{B_{S_{1}}^{2\vartheta_{1}}},\frac{1}{B_{S_{2}}^{2\vartheta_{2}}},\frac{1}{B_{T}^{2\vartheta_{3}}}\right\},

where
\vartheta_{1}
,
\vartheta_{2}
, and
\vartheta_{3}
are defined in Assumption
6.5
.

Our results are nonstandard in the literature on geostatistics: we do not need to choose between in-fill or long-span asymptotic regime and we simply require that both
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
diverge, so
S\to\infty
. With this regard, we emphasize that our estimator of the spectral density matrix entries as in (
17
) bears some similarities with the tapered estimator of the Fourier transform of the covariance matrix of a spatial rf on a lattice proposed by
Dahlhaus and Künsch 1987
. Differently from their method, in our approach we replace data tapers with kernels. This yields a two-fold advantage: first, it allows to control for the estimation bias of
\sigma_{ij}^{x}(\bm{\theta})
, taking care of the boundary effects; second, it offers the possibility of using the mentioned flexible asymptotic regime. We refer to
El Machkouri, Volnỳ and
Wu 2013
for a related discussion; see also
Deb, Pourahmadi and Wu 2017
for similar comments.

Remark 6.3
.

The rate in Theorem
6.1
depends on the kernel smoothness
\vartheta_{l}
,
l=1,2,3
and the bandwidths
B_{S_{1}}
,
B_{S_{2}}
, and
B_{T}
(see Assumption
6.5
). Typically the same kernel is used in all dimensions, so we can assume
\vartheta_{l}=\vartheta_{o}
for all
l=1,2,3
.
Consider the case in which
S_{1}\asymp S_{2}\asymp T
, then, up to logarithmic terms, the optimal spatial bandwidths are such that

B_{S_{1}}\asymp S_{1}^{3/(2\vartheta_{o}+3)}
,
B_{S_{2}}\asymp S_{2}^{3/(2\vartheta_{o}+3)}
, and
B_{T}\asymp T^{3/(2\vartheta_{o}+3)}
.
This implies that the optimal rate of consistency for our estimator of the spectral density matrix is
S_{1}^{3\vartheta_{o}/(2\vartheta_{o}+3)}=S_{2}^{3\vartheta_{o}/(2\vartheta_{o}+3)}=T^{3\vartheta_{o}/(2\vartheta_{o}+3)}
. In our applications we used the Epanechnikov kernel for which
\vartheta_{o}=2
, hence, the rate of consistency is
S_{1}^{6/7}=S_{2}^{6/7}=T^{6/7}
.
In a pure time series model the consistency rate is
T^{\vartheta_{o}/(2\vartheta_{o}+1)}

(
Barigozzi and
Farnè 2022
)
, which for a Epanechnikov kernel implies a rate
T^{2/5}
, much slower than what achieved using also the spatial information.

We then prove consistency of the common component estimator
\widehat{\chi}_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}^{(n)}
defined in (
20
)

Theorem 6.2
.

Let Assumptions
3.1
,
3.2
,
6.1
,
6.2
,
6.3
,
6.4
,
6.5
, and
6.6
hold.
Define

\alpha_{n,S_{1},S_{2},T}=\max\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},(\log B_{S_{1}}\log B_{S_{2}}\log B_{T})\sqrt{\frac{B_{S_{1}}B_{S_{2}}B_{T}}{S_{1}S_{2}T}},\frac{1}{B_{S_{1}}^{\vartheta_{1}}},\frac{1}{B_{S_{2}}^{\vartheta_{2}}},\frac{1}{B_{T}^{\vartheta_{3}}}\right\},

where
\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}
, and
\vartheta_{3}
are defined in Assumption
6.5
. Then,
there exists finite
{C},C^{*},\widetilde{C}>0
independent of
n,S_{1},S_{2}
, and
T
, such that,

(i)

for any
\bm{\varsigma}=(s_{1}\ s_{2}\ t)^{\top}
with
s_{1}=1,\ldots,S_{1}
,
s_{2}=1,\ldots,S_{2}
, and
t=1,\ldots,T
, and for all
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq\ell\leq n}{\rm P}\left[\left|\widehat{\chi}^{(n)}_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}-\chi_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}\right|\geq\varepsilon\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\alpha_{n,S_{1},S_{2},T}M_{S_{1}}M_{S_{2}}M_{T}

\displaystyle+\frac{C^{*}}{\varepsilon}(1+\varepsilon_{1})^{-\kappa^{*}_{1}(s_{1})}(1+\varepsilon_{2})^{-\kappa^{*}_{2}(s_{2})}(1+\varepsilon_{3})^{-\kappa^{*}_{3}(t)},

with
\kappa^{*}_{1}(s_{1})=\min\{|\underline{\kappa}_{1}(s_{1})-1|,\overline{\kappa}_{1}(s_{1})+1\}
,
\kappa^{*}_{2}(s_{2})=\min\{|\underline{\kappa}_{2}(s_{2})-1|,\overline{\kappa}_{2}(s_{2})+1\}
and
\kappa^{*}_{3}(t)=\min\{|\underline{\kappa}_{3}(t)-1|,\overline{\kappa}_{3}(t)+1\}
, and where

M_{S_{1}}
,
M_{S_{2}}
, and
M_{T}
are defined in Assumption
6.5
,

\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}
, and
\varepsilon_{3}
are defined in Assumption
6.6
, and

\underline{\kappa}_{1}(s_{1}),\overline{\kappa}_{1}(s_{1}),\underline{\kappa}_{2}(s_{2}),\overline{\kappa}_{2}(s_{2}),\underline{\kappa}_{3}(t)
, and
\overline{\kappa}_{3}(t)
are defined in (
19
).

(ii)

for any
\bm{\varsigma}=(s_{1}\ s_{2}\ t)^{\top}
with
s_{1}=M_{S_{1}},\ldots,S_{1}-M_{S_{1}}
and
s_{2}=M_{S_{2}},\ldots,S_{2}-M_{S_{2}}
and
t=M_{T},\ldots,T-M_{T}
, and for all
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq\ell\leq n}{\rm P}\left[\left|\widehat{\chi}^{(n)}_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}-\chi_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}\right|\geq\varepsilon\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\widetilde{C}}{\varepsilon}\alpha_{n,S_{1},S_{2},T}M_{S_{1}}M_{S_{2}}M_{T}.

Theorem
6.2
proves that for consistency of
\widehat{\chi}^{(n)}_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}
, the number of lags
M_{S_{1}}
,
M_{S_{2}}
, and
M_{T}
used in (
18
) should not be too large, while
n
,
S_{1}
,
S_{2}
and
T
should all diverge to infinity. As it is clear from Theorem
5.1
, we need a large
n
to disentangle the common and the idiosyncratic components, while from Theorem
6.1
we see that we need large
S_{1}
,
S_{2}
, and
T
to consistently estimate the spectral density matrix of the observed rf. We remark that part (i) yields a rate of convergence also when the spatial locations and the time are close to the boundaries: this aspects has been neglected in the literature on factors models.

Remark 6.4
.

The consistency rate depends on the truncation level we choose when applying the two-sided filter in (
18
). When considering the same setting as in Remark
6.3
so that the consistency rate for the estimate spectral density is
T^{6/7}
, and assuming
M_{S_{1}}=M_{S_{2}}=M_{T}=M
, we need
M=o(T^{2/7})
.

7
Determining the number of factors

An essential aspect for the implementation of the GSTFM is the correct identification of the number of factors
q
. Theorem
4.1
provides a rough guideline for this: intuitively, one should choose the value of
q
such that the
q
-th dynamic eigenvalue should be “sufficiently large" while the
q+1
-th one should not be “small". To provide a more precise selection procedure, we define an information criterion (IC) that enables us to estimate
q
consistently. To this end,
we propose the use of a criterion which is based on the eigenvalues,
\widehat{\lambda}^{x}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})
,
j=1,\ldots,n
, of
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}^{x}(\bm{\theta})
.

Letting
p(n,S_{1},S_{2},T)
denote a penalty depending on both
n
and on
S_{1},S_{2}
, and
T
, we consider the information criterion

\widehat{\rm IC}^{(n)}(k)=\log\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=k+1}^{n}\frac{1}{8\pi^{3}}\int_{\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}}\widehat{\lambda}^{x}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})\mathrm{d}\bm{\theta}\right]+k\,p(n,S_{1},S_{2},T),

and we define the estimator of the number of factors

\widehat{q}^{(n)}=\arg\!\!\!\!\!\!\min_{0\leq k\leq q_{\max}}\widehat{\rm IC}^{(n)}(k),

(22)

for some a priori chosen maximum number of factors
q_{\max}
.
We assume the following standard divergence rate of the penalty

Assumption 7.1
.

As
n,S_{1},S_{2},T\rightarrow\infty
,

p(n,S_{1},S_{2},T)\rightarrow 0
and

\min\left\{n,\frac{1}{\log B_{S_{1}}\log B_{S_{2}}\log B_{T}}\sqrt{\frac{S_{1}S_{2}T}{B_{S_{1}}B_{S_{2}}B_{T}}},B_{S_{1}}^{\vartheta_{1}},B_{S_{2}}^{\vartheta_{2}},B_{T}^{\vartheta_{3}}\right\}p(n,S_{1},S_{2},T)\rightarrow\infty.

Finally, we establish consistency of
\widehat{q}^{(n)}

Theorem 7.1
.

Let Assumptions
3.1
,
3.2
,
6.1
,
6.2
,
6.3
,
6.4
,
6.5
, and
7.1
hold.
Then, as
n,S_{1},S_{2},T\rightarrow\infty
,
{\rm P}(\widehat{q}^{(n)}=q)\to 1
.

8
Monte Carlo experiments

Before delving into numerical studies, we summarize the estimation procedure in the following

Input:

data
\{x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}},\ \ell=1,\ldots,n,\bm{\varsigma}=(s_{1}\ s_{2}\ t)^{\top},s_{1}=1,\ldots,S_{1},s_{2}=1,\ldots,S_{2},t=1,\ldots,T\}
;
estimated number of factors
\widehat{q}^{(n)}
(see Algorithm
2
in Appendix
G
); kernel functions
K_{1}(\cdot)
,
K_{2}(\cdot)
, and
K_{3}(\cdot)
;bandwidths integers
B_{S_{1}},B_{S_{2}}
, and
B_{T}
; truncation integers
M_{S_{1}}
,
M_{S_{2}}
, and
M_{T}
.

Output:

\{\widehat{\chi}_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}^{(n)}
,
\ell=1,\ldots,n
,
\bm{\varsigma}=(s_{1}\ s_{2}\ t)^{\top},s_{1}=1,\ldots,S_{1},s_{2}=1,\ldots,S_{2},t=1,\ldots,T\}
.

1

Compute
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}^{x}(\bm{\theta}_{\bm{h}})
as in (
17
), with
\bm{\theta}_{\bm{h}}
as in Remark
6.1
.

2

Compute the
\widehat{q}^{(n)}
eigenvectors
\widehat{{\bm{p}}}_{nj}^{x}(\bm{\theta}_{\bm{h}}),j=1,\ldots,\widehat{q}
, of
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}^{x}(\bm{\theta}_{\bm{h}})
, with
\bm{\theta}_{\bm{h}}
as in Remark
6.1
.

3

Compute
\widehat{{\bm{K}}}^{x}_{n\ell}(\bm{\theta}_{\bm{h}})
and
\widehat{\underline{{\bm{K}}}}^{x,\bm{\varsigma}}_{n\ell}(L)
as in (
18
), with
\bm{\theta}_{\bm{h}}
as in Remark
6.1
.

4

Compute
\widehat{\chi}^{(n)}_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}=\widehat{\underline{{\bm{K}}}}^{x,\bm{\varsigma}}_{n\ell}(L){\bm{x}}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}
as in (
20
).

5

Algorithm 1

Algorithm for estimating the common component.

We illustrate how Algorithm
1
works and we provide evidence of our key theoretical results. In Section
2
we already showed the presence of the eigen-gap in finite-samples as predicted by our results in Section
4
, further evidence is available in Appendix
I
; in Section
8.1
, we study the performance of the estimator of the common component proposed in Section
6
, and we provide a comparison of our GSTFM with the extant GDFM; in Section
8.2
, we explain how to select the number of factors following Section
7
.

In the whole section we simulate data using
x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}=\chi_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}+\xi_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}
, for
\ell=1,\ldots,n
,
\bm{\varsigma}=(s_{1}\ s_{2}\ t)^{\top}
with
s_{1}=1,\ldots,S_{1}
,
s_{2}=1,\ldots,S_{2}
, and
t=1,\ldots,T
. The case of cross- and serially correlated idiosyncratic components is studied in Appendix
I
.
The idiosyncratic component
\xi_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}
is i.i.d. from a standard normal distribution and the common component
\chi_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}
is generated according to two different mechanisms.

Model (a)

is an infinite convolution over the lattice:

\displaystyle\chi_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}=\sum_{\bm{\kappa}}\sum_{j=1}^{q}a_{\ell j}b_{\ell j}^{|\kappa_{1}|+|\kappa_{2}|+|\kappa_{3}|}L^{\bm{\kappa}}{u}_{j,\bm{\varsigma}}.

(23)

Model (b)

is a finite convolution over the lattice:

\chi_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}=\sum_{\bm{\kappa}=(-1\ -1\ 0)^{\top}}^{(1\ 1\ 1)^{\top}}\sum_{j=1}^{q}a_{\ell j}0.5^{|\kappa_{1}|+|\kappa_{2}|+|\kappa_{3}|}L^{\bm{\kappa}}{u}_{j,\bm{\varsigma}}.

(24)

We generate

a_{\ell j}
and
u_{j,\bm{\varsigma}}
,
j=1,\ldots,q,
from i.i.d. standard normal distributions and
b_{\ell j}
from i.i.d. uniform distributions on
[0.5,0.8]
. The Monte Carlo (MC) experiments are repeated
N=100
times.

8.1
The common component

Section
6
contains the asymptotics of the proposed estimation methods. A practically relevant question is related to the finite-sample behaviour of the proposed estimators. To investigate this aspect, we set
q=2
and we study numerically how the mean square error (MSE)

E_{1}=\frac{1}{nS_{1}S_{2}T}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\sum_{s_{1}=1}^{S_{1}}\sum_{s_{2}=1}^{S_{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\widehat{\chi}^{(n)}_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}-\chi_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}})^{2}

and the standardised MSE

E_{2}=\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\sum_{s_{1}=1}^{S_{1}}\sum_{s_{2}=1}^{S_{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\widehat{\chi}^{(n)}_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}-\chi_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}})^{2}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\sum_{s_{1}=1}^{S_{1}}\sum_{s_{2}=1}^{S_{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\chi_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}^{2}}

change with
n
and with the spatio-temporal dimensions
S_{1},S_{2}
and
T
.

In the top panel of Table
1
, we display the averaged (over all MC runs)
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
for
n=20,40,60,80
and
(S_{1},S_{2},T)=(20,20,20)
. The table clearly shows that the estimation errors decrease as
n
increases: this illustrates the blessing of dimensionality for the estimation of the common component. Interestingly, we remark that already with
n=20
,
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
have values that are very similar to the ones obtained for larger sample sizes (e.g.
n=60
).

In the bottom panel of Table
1
we report the averaged (over all MC runs) values of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
for
n=40
and
(S_{1},S_{2},T)=(10,10,10)d,
with
d=1,2,3,4
. In line with the theoretical results, the errors decrease as the spatio-temporal dimensions increase.

To elaborate on the motivating example of Section
2
, we compare the performance of the GSTFM and the GDFM in terms of estimation accuracy of the common components. We set
n=30
,
q=2
and
(S_{1},S_{2},T)=(10,10,20)
or
(S_{1},S_{2},T)=(20,10,20)
. In Table
2
, we report the average (over all MC runs) values of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, for the GSTFM and GDFM. The advantage of our approach is evident: the GSTFM produces smaller estimation errors of the common components than the GDFM. We emphasize that
E_{1}
of the GDFM displays a sharp rise as
S_{1}
increases from
10
to
20
. This aspect illustrates that there is no blessing of dimensionality for the GDFM if the spatial dependencies are ignored: adding more time series does not yield any accuracy improvement and the results of
Forni et al. 2000
do not apply. Indeed,
when
S_{1}
increases, stacking the new observations in a vector, as in Section
2
, implies that we are dealing with a larger number of spatially dependent variables: the GDFM ignores these spatial dependencies and, as a result, it becomes less reliable in the estimation of the common component, entailing larger values of
E_{1}
—incidentally, this point is not detectable looking at
E_{2}
because of its standardisation based on the variance of the true common component.

8.2
Selection of the number of factors

We investigate the finite sample performance of the estimator of
q
defined in Section
7
. However, looking at (
22
), we remark that, if the estimator
\widehat{q}^{(n)}
is consistent, then the estimator
\widehat{q}_{c}^{(n)}
obtained via the penalty
cp(n,S_{1},S_{2},T)
,
c>0
, is consistent as well. Hence, in practice, one needs to choose also
c
to estimate
q
consistently. The detailed procedure for the automatic selection of the number of factors is summarized in Algorithm
2
in Appendix
G
.
To evaluate the estimation accuracy of Algorithm
2
, we set
n=100
,
(S_{1},S_{2},T)=(25,25,25)
, and

q=0,1,2,3
and we run
200
MC replications. Table
3
shows the under- and over-identification proportions for
\widehat{q}_{\widehat{c}}^{(n)}
. The results illustrate good finite-sample performance of the selection procedure of
q
: for Model (b) in (
24
), the algorithm identifies
q
correctly for all replications and for all values of
q
; for Model (a) in (
23
), the over-identification rate is not zero for
q=1,2,3
but it is nevertheless very small.

9
Conclusions and further developments

We develop the theory and provide the complete inference toolkit (estimation of the common component and selection of the number of factors) for the factor analysis of high-dimensional
spatio-temporal rf defined on a lattice. Our model accounts for all spatio-temporal common correlations among all components of the rf. We give statistical guarantees of the proposed estimation methods.
Our asymptotic theory extends the one available in
Forni et al. 2000
, whose rates of convergence, which are unavailable in the literature on factor models for time series, can be derived as a special case of our rates in Section
6
. Monte Carlo studies illustrate the applicability and the good performance
of our GSTFM under many different settings, commonly encountered in data analysis.

We foresee some
extensions of our results. For instance, one may define estimators of the common component which involve one-sided filters in time, thus allowing for forecasting. We conjecture that this is possible along the lines of
(
Forni et al. 2005
;
Forni et al. 2017
)
. Nevertheless, such extensions cannot be directly obtained within the setting of this paper: they require further assumptions and more involved estimation steps, whose statistical guarantees need to be derived. Therefore, we leave them for further research.

†
†
funding:
The third author was supported by grants WK2040000055 and YD2040002016.

{supplement}

Appendix A
Preparatory results on infinite dimensional random fields

A.1
Lag operator in space-time

Lemma A.1
.

Under Assumption
3.1
, for any
j=1,2,3
, the operator
L_{j}
in (
1
) can be straightforwardly and uniquely extended to
L_{j}:\bm{\mathcal{X}}\to\bm{\mathcal{X}}
which is well defined, preserves the inner product and is onto. Thus,
L_{j}
is a unitary operator.

Proof.

Let
\{\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}\}
and
\{\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}}\}
be sequences of complex numbers. First, we show that the operator
L_{1}
is well-defined. To this end, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we notice that for each
\bm{\varsigma}=(s_{1}\ s_{2}\ t)^{\top}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, the linearity of the operator yields, for a finite linear combination,

\displaystyle L_{1}\left[\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}\right]=\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}x_{\ell(s_{1}-1\ s_{2}\ t)}.

Let us recall that
L_{2}(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{C})
is a Hilbert space with inner product
\langle x_{i\bm{\varsigma}},x_{j\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}\rangle=\textrm{Cov}(x_{i\bm{\varsigma}},x_{j\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}})
and
\|{x_{i\bm{\varsigma}}}\|^{2}=\langle x_{i\bm{\varsigma}},x_{i\bm{\varsigma}}\rangle
.
Now, we remark that if

\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}=\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}}x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}},

then we have

\displaystyle\left\|L_{1}\left[\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}\right]-L_{1}\left[\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}}x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}\right]\right\|^{2}=\left\|\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}x_{\ell(s_{1}-1\ s_{2}\ t)}-\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}}x_{\ell(s_{1}-1\ s_{2}\ t)}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}(\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}-\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}}){(\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}^{\dagger}-\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}^{\dagger})}\left\langle x_{\ell(s_{1}-1\ s_{2}\ t)},x_{\ell(s^{\prime}_{1}-1\ s^{\prime}_{2}\ t^{\prime})}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}(\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}-\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}}){(\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}^{\dagger}-\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}^{\dagger})}\left\langle x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}},x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}-\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}}x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}\right\|^{2}=0,

where, in the third line, we made use of the homostationarity as in Assumption
3.1
. Thus, the operator is well defined.
Now, we show that
L_{1}
preserves the inner product. For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, consider

\displaystyle\left\langle L_{1}\left[\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}\right],L_{1}\left[\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}}x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}\right]\right\rangle=\left\langle\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}x_{\ell(s_{1}-1\ s_{2}\ t)},\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}}x_{\ell(s_{1}-1\ s_{2}\ t)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\left\langle x_{\ell(s_{1}-1\ s_{2}\ t)},x_{\ell(s^{\prime}_{1}-1\ s^{\prime}_{2}\ t^{\prime})}\right\rangle=\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\left\langle x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}},x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}},\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}{\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}}x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}\right\rangle,

where in the fourth equality we made use of homostationarity as in Assumption
3.1
. As a consequence
L_{1}
is bounded. It is straightforward to show that
L_{1}
is onto, therefore it is unitary, which implies that its inverse
L^{-1}_{1}
coincides with the adjoint operator, say
L_{1}^{\dagger}
, and
L_{1}^{\dagger}L_{1}=I
, where
I
is the identity operator.

The above properties hold for
L_{1}
applied just to one
x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}
, but, since
L_{1}
is linear the same hold when applying
L_{1}
to elements of
\cup_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
. We can then extend
L_{1}
to
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
as follows. Let
\zeta\in\bm{\mathcal{X}}
then there must exist a sequence
\{\zeta_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\zeta_{n}\in\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{n}

such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\big\|\zeta-\zeta_{n}\big\|=0.

Therefore,
\{\zeta_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence and since
L_{1}
preserves the norm we have that
\{L_{1}\zeta_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is also a Cauchy sequence and must converge to an element
\eta\in\bm{\mathcal{X}}
, i.e.
\lim_{n\to\infty}\big\|\eta-L_{1}\zeta_{n}\big\|=0,

and it must be that
\eta=L_{1}\zeta
in order for
L_{1}
to be norm preserving. Using the linearity and boundedness of the operator and continuity of the inner product, it is then easy to show that the operator
L_{1}
extended in this way to
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
is still linear, well defined and it preserves inner product. We still denote by
L_{1}
the extended operator. Finally, note that
L_{1}
is unitary on
\cup_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
, we have
\zeta_{n}=L_{1}^{\dagger}L_{1}\zeta_{n}
and therefore for any
\zeta\in\bm{\mathcal{X}}
there exists an
\eta\in\bm{\mathcal{X}}
such that
L_{1}\eta=\zeta
, which shows that
L_{1}
extended to
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
is onto.

Moving along the same lines of this proof, one can verify that the same reasoning applies also for the operators
L_{2}
and
L_{3}
. That concludes the proof.
∎

A.2
Canonical isomorphism

Consider generic infinite dimensional row vectors of functions
\bm{f}=(f_{1}\ f_{2}\ \cdots f_{\ell}\cdots)
such that
f_{\ell}:\bm{\Theta}\to\mathbb{C}
is measurable for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and with
n
dimensional row sub-vectors
\bm{f}_{n}=(f_{1}\cdots f_{n})
. We define the complex linear spaces

(i)

L^{\infty}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C},\bm{\Sigma}^{x})
of all
\bm{f}
such that

\|\bm{f}\|_{\bm{\Sigma}^{x}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\bm{f}_{n}\|_{\bm{\Sigma}^{x}}<\infty
, where
\|\bm{f}\|_{\bm{\Sigma}^{x}}=\sqrt{\langle\bm{f},\bm{f}\rangle_{\bm{\Sigma}^{x}}}
with
the inner product is given by

\langle\bm{f},\bm{g}\rangle_{\bm{\Sigma}^{x}}=\int_{\bm{\Theta}}\bm{f}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Sigma}^{x}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{g}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})\mathrm{d}\bm{\theta}/{8\pi^{3}}
.

(ii)

L^{\infty}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C})\equiv L^{\infty}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C},\mathbf{I})

where
\mathbf{I}
is the infinite dimensional identity matrix (namely, the matrix having
\mathbf{I}_{n}
as the
n\times n
top-left sub-matrix). On
L^{\infty}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C})
, the inner product and the norm are indicated by
\langle\bm{f},\bm{g}\rangle
and
\|\bm{f}\|
, respectively.

(iii)

L^{n}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C},\bm{\Sigma}_{n}^{x})
and
L^{n}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C})
as
L^{\infty}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C},\bm{\Sigma}^{x})
and
L^{\infty}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C})
, but with the
n
-dimensional vectors
\bm{f}_{n}
instead of
\bm{f}
.

(iv)

L_{\infty}^{n}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C})
of all
\bm{f}_{n}
such that
g=\|{\bm{f}_{n}}\|
is essentially bounded, i.e.,
\text{ess}\sup(g)<\infty
, where
\text{ess}\sup(g)=\inf\{M:\mathcal{L}[y:g(y)>M]=0\}
.

Notice that
L^{\infty}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C},\bm{\Sigma}^{x})
,
L^{n}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C},\bm{\Sigma}^{x})
,
L^{\infty}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C})
, and
L^{n}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C})
are Hilbert spaces.

Lemma A.2
.

Under Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
, the map
\mathcal{J}
in (
2
) can be straightforwardly and uniquely extended to a map
\mathcal{J}:L^{\infty}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C},\bm{\Sigma}^{x})\to\bm{\mathcal{X}}
which is well defined, preserves the inner product and is one-to-one. Thus,
\mathcal{J}
is an isomorphism.
Moreover,
let
L_{2}^{n*}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C},\bm{\Sigma}^{x})=\{\bm{f}\in L_{2}^{\infty}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C},\bm{\Sigma}^{x}),f_{j}=0,j>n\}
. For any
\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
and any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and for a given integer
\ell\leq n
,
define the mapping
\mathcal{J}^{*}:\cup_{n=1}^{\infty}L_{2}^{n*}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C},\bm{\Sigma}^{x})\to\cup_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{\mathcal{X}}_{n}

as:

\mathcal{J}^{*}\left[(\delta_{\ell 1}\ \cdots\delta_{\ell k}\cdots\delta_{\ell n})e^{i\langle\bm{\varsigma},\cdot\rangle}\right]=x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}},

The map
\mathcal{J}^{*}
can be extended in a unique way to
\mathcal{J}:L^{\infty}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C},\bm{\Sigma}^{x})\to\bm{\mathcal{X}}
which is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We show that
\mathcal{J}
is an isometric
isomorphism since it is onto, it preserves the inner product and it is one-to-one.

Let us set
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
and
\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
.
From (
2
), we have
\mathcal{J}\left[(\delta_{\ell 1}\ \cdots\ \delta_{\ell n})e^{i\langle\bm{\varsigma},\cdot\rangle}\right]=x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}
, by linearity and for a sequence of complex numbers
\{\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}\}
, we have

\mathcal{J}\left[\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}(\delta_{\ell 1}\ \cdots\ \delta_{\ell n})e^{i\langle\bm{\varsigma},\cdot\rangle}\right]=\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}},

which is an onto linear mapping between two spaces: the collection of finite combinations of
\{(\delta_{\ell 1}\ \cdots\ \delta_{\ell n})e^{i\langle\bm{\varsigma},\cdot\rangle}:\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\}
and all finite linear combinations of
\{x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}:\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\}
. We remark that these two spaces are dense manifolds of
L^{\infty}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C},\bm{\Sigma}^{x})
and
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
, respectively.

To show that
\mathcal{J}
preserves the inner product, let us consider the complex sequences
\{\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}\}

and
\{\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}\}
and define the
n
dimensional vector
\bm{A}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}=(0\ \ \cdots\ \alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}\ \cdots\ 0)^{\top}
, which has all entries equal to zero, but the
\ell
-th entry which is equal to
\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}
. Similarly, we define the
n
dimensional vector
\bm{B}_{n\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}=(0\ \ \cdots\ \beta_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}\ \cdots\ 0)^{\top}
, which has all entries equal to zero, but the
\ell
-th entry which is equal to
\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{J}\left[\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}(\delta_{\ell 1}\ \cdots\ \delta_{\ell n})e^{i\langle\bm{\varsigma},\cdot\rangle}\right],\mathcal{J}\left[\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}}(\delta_{\ell 1}\ \cdots\ \delta_{\ell n})e^{i\langle\bm{\varsigma},\cdot\rangle}\right]\right\rangle

(A.1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}{x}_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}},\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}}{x}_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}\right\rangle=\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}{\beta^{{\dagger}}_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}}\left\langle{x}_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}},{x}_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}\bm{A}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}\bm{\Gamma}^{x}_{n}(\bm{\varsigma}-\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}){\bm{B}_{n\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8\pi^{3}}\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}\bm{A}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}\left[\int_{\bm{\Theta}}e^{i\langle\bm{\varsigma}-\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime},\bm{\theta}\rangle}\bm{\Sigma}_{n}^{x}(\bm{\theta})d\bm{\theta}\right]{\bm{B}_{n\bm{\varsigma}^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}\alpha_{\bm{\varsigma}}e^{i\langle\bm{\varsigma},\bm{\theta}\rangle},\sum_{\bm{\varsigma}}{\beta_{\bm{\varsigma}}}e^{i\langle\bm{\varsigma},\bm{\theta}\rangle}\right\rangle_{\bm{\Sigma}_{n}^{x}(\bm{\theta})},

which implies that
\mathcal{J}
is a one-to-one mapping and it preserves the inner product.
The above properties hold for
\mathcal{J}
applied just to
(\delta_{\ell 1}\ \cdots\ \delta_{\ell n})e^{i\langle\bm{\varsigma},\cdot\rangle}
, which yields
x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}
. Nevertheless,
\mathcal{J}
is linear thus the same properties hold when applying it to get all the elements of
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
.

We have shown that
\mathcal{J}
is an isometric isomorphism between two dense linear manifolds. Lemma 4.1 in
Rozanov 1967
implies that one can always extend the isomorphism to the closed linear manifolds generated by
these manifolds. With a slight abuse of notation, we call
\mathcal{J}
the extended isomorphism such that
\mathcal{J}:L^{\infty}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C},\bm{\Sigma}^{x})\to\bm{\mathcal{X}}
.
That concludes the proof.
∎

Remark A.1
.

A consequence of Lemma
A.2
is that
also the inverse mapping of
\mathcal{J}
, let us call it
\mathcal{J}^{-1}:\bm{\mathcal{X}}\to L^{\infty}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C},\bm{\Sigma}^{x})
, is an isomorphism, such that for
\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and any
\ell\leq n
,

\mathcal{J}^{-1}\left[x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}\right]=(\delta_{\ell 1}\ \cdots\delta_{\ell k}\cdots\delta_{\ell n})e^{i\langle\bm{\varsigma},\cdot\rangle}.

(A.2)

Moreover, Lemma
A.2
implies that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the process
\bm{x}_{n}
is harmonizable, namely
for any
\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, we can write

\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}=\int_{\bm{\Theta}}e^{i\langle\bm{\varsigma},\bm{\theta}\rangle}\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{n}(\bm{\theta})\mathrm{d}\bm{\theta},\hskip 18.49988pt\text{w.p. 1},

(A.3)

where
\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
is a complex random measure on (the Borel
\sigma
-field of)
\bm{\Theta}
, which is such that

\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{n}(\bm{\Delta_{1}}\cup\bm{\Delta_{2}})=\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{n}(\bm{\Delta_{1}})+\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{n}(\bm{\Delta_{2}})
, for disjoint Borel sets
\bm{\Delta_{1}}
and
\bm{\Delta_{2}}
. In addition, for any Borel set
\bm{\Delta}
, we have that
\textrm{E}(\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{n}(\bm{\Delta}))=\bm{0}_{n}
,
\textrm{E}(|\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{n}(\bm{\Delta})|^{2})=\int_{\bm{\Delta}}\bm{\Sigma}_{n}^{x}(d\bm{\theta})/8\pi^{3}
and
\textrm{E}(\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{n}(\bm{\Delta_{1}})\bm{\mathcal{M}}^{{\dagger}}_{n}(\bm{\Delta_{2}}))=\bm{0}_{n\times n}
, for all disjoint Borel sets

\bm{\Delta_{1}}
and
\bm{\Delta_{2}}
; see, e.g.,
Stein 2012
, p.21 or
Cressie and Wikle 2015
Ch.6, section 6.1.6, for book-length discussions. The result in (
A.3
) is analogous to the standard spectral representation of time series, where
\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
is the measure related to an orthogonal increment process; see e.g.
Brockwell and Davis 2006
, Ch.4.

Remark A.2
.

To understand the importance of linear filter, notice that any scalar rf of the form
y_{\bm{\varsigma}}=\bm{\underline{b}}(L)\bm{x}_{\bm{\varsigma}}
is such that
y\in\bm{\mathcal{X}}
and therefore is co-homostationary with
x_{\ell}
for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.
Moreover,
y
has a scalar spectral density
\sigma^{y}(\bm{\theta})
. In particular, recalling the definition of inner product in the Hilbert space
L_{2}(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{C})
, we have
by Lemma
A.2
and (
7
), for any
\bm{\varsigma},\bm{h}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}

\displaystyle\textrm{E}(y_{\bm{\varsigma}}y_{\bm{\varsigma}-\bm{h}}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\langle y_{\bm{\varsigma}},y_{\bm{\varsigma}-\bm{h}}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{J}^{-1}[y_{\bm{\varsigma}}],\mathcal{J}^{-1}[y_{\bm{\varsigma}-\bm{h}}]\rangle_{\bm{\Sigma}^{x}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi^{3}}\int_{\bm{\Theta}}e^{i\langle\bm{\varsigma},\bm{\theta}\rangle}\left[\left(\sum_{\bm{\kappa}}\mathbf{b}_{\bm{\kappa}}e^{-i\langle\bm{\kappa},\bm{\theta}\rangle}\right)\bm{\Sigma}^{x}(\bm{\theta})\left(\sum_{\bm{\kappa}}\mathbf{b}_{\bm{\kappa}}e^{-i\langle\bm{\kappa},\bm{\theta}\rangle}\right)^{\dagger}\right]e^{-i\langle\bm{\varsigma}-\bm{h},\bm{\theta}\rangle}\mathrm{d}\bm{\theta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi^{3}}\int_{\bm{\Theta}}\left[\left(\sum_{\bm{\kappa}}\mathbf{b}_{\bm{\kappa}}e^{-i\langle\bm{\kappa},\bm{\theta}\rangle}\right)\bm{\Sigma}^{x}(\bm{\theta})\left(\sum_{\bm{\kappa}}\mathbf{b}_{\bm{\kappa}}e^{-i\langle\bm{\kappa},\bm{\theta}\rangle}\right)^{\dagger}\right]e^{i\langle\bm{h},\bm{\theta}\rangle}\mathrm{d}\bm{\theta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi^{3}}\int_{\bm{\Theta}}\sigma^{y}(\bm{\theta})e^{i\langle\bm{h},\bm{\theta}\rangle}\mathrm{d}\bm{\theta},

which shows that, for any
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
, the spectral density of
y
is

\sigma^{y}(\bm{\theta})=\left(\sum_{\bm{\kappa}}\mathbf{b}_{\bm{\kappa}}e^{-i\langle\bm{\kappa},\bm{\theta}\rangle}\right)\bm{\Sigma}^{x}(\bm{\theta})\left(\sum_{\bm{\kappa}}\mathbf{b}_{\bm{\kappa}}e^{-i\langle\bm{\kappa},\bm{\theta}\rangle}\right)^{\dagger}=\left(\sum_{\bm{\kappa}}\mathbf{b}_{\bm{\kappa}}e^{-i\langle\bm{\kappa},\bm{\theta}\rangle}\right)\bm{\Sigma}^{x}(\bm{\theta})\left(\sum_{\bm{\kappa}}\mathbf{b}_{\bm{\kappa}}e^{i\langle\bm{\kappa},\bm{\theta}\rangle}\right).

The same reasoning can be easily generalized to the case of
m
-dimensional linear filters, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, defining filtered rf as
\bm{y}_{m\bm{\varsigma}}=\underline{\bm{B}}(L)\bm{x}_{\bm{\varsigma}}=\sum_{\bm{\kappa}}\bm{B}_{\bm{\kappa}}L^{\bm{\kappa}}\bm{x}_{\bm{\varsigma}}
, where
\bm{B}_{\bm{\kappa}}
is a complex matrix with
m
rows and infinite columns and such that
\bm{y}_{m\bm{\varsigma}}\in\bm{\mathcal{X}}
for any
\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
. For
j=1,\ldots,m
, each component
y_{j\bm{\varsigma}}=\underline{\bm{b}}_{j}(L)\bm{x}_{\bm{\varsigma}}
satisfies the above properties.

Remark A.3
.

If
\bm{x}_{n}
is a white noise rf for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then it has a constant spectral density matrix.

Appendix B
Proof of results of Section 4

B.1
Weyl’s inequality

For the sake of completeness, we recall some properties of the eigenvalues of Hermitian nonnegative definite matrices, that go under the name of Weyl’s inequality.

(a)

Let
\mathbf{D}
and
\mathbf{E}
be
m\times m
Hermitian nonnegative definite and
\bm{F}=\bm{D}+\bm{E}
. Then

\nu_{s}(\bm{F})\leq\nu_{s}(\bm{D})+\nu_{1}(\bm{E}),\hskip 9.24994pt\nu_{s}(\bm{F})\leq\nu_{1}(\bm{D})+\nu_{s}(\bm{E}),\hskip 9.24994pt\nu_{s}(\bm{F})\geq\nu_{s}(\bm{D}),\hskip 9.24994pt\nu_{s}(\bm{F})\geq\nu_{s}(\bm{E})

for any
s=1,\ldots,m
.

(b)

Let
\mathbf{D}
be as in (a) and let
\mathbf{G}
be the top-left
(m-1)\times(m-1)
submatrix of
\bm{D}
. Then
\nu_{s}(\bm{D})\geq\nu_{s}(\bm{G})
for
s=1,\ldots,m-1
.

B.2
Proof of Theorem
4.1
- sufficient condition

The proof of the sufficient condition is based on a series of intermediate results. In the sequel, for ease of notation, when we write matrix products
\bm{A}\bm{B}
but the number of columns of
\bm{A}
is smaller than the number of rows of
\bm{B}
we mean that
\bm{A}
has been augmented with columns of zeros to match the number of rows of
\bm{B}
. For example, for
m<n
,
\underline{\bm{P}}_{m}(L)\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}
means nothing but
\underline{\bm{P}}_{m}(L)\bm{x}_{m\bm{\varsigma}}
.

B.2.1
Construction of
q
-dimensional orthonormal white noise rf

We start proving that there exists a converging sequence of
q
-dimensional orthonormal white noise rf which belongs to
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
.
For a given integer
q\leq n
and any
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
, let
\bm{P}_{n}(\bm{\theta})=(\bm{p}_{n1}^{x\top}(\bm{\theta})\cdots\bm{p}_{nq}^{x\top}(\bm{\theta}))^{\top}
be the
q\times n
matrix having as rows the normalized eigenvectors of
\bm{\Sigma}_{n}^{x}(\bm{\theta})
corresponding to the
q
largest eigenvectors. Similarly let
\bm{Q}_{n}(\bm{\theta})=(\bm{p}_{nq+1}^{x\top}(\bm{\theta})\cdots\bm{p}_{nn}^{x\top}(\bm{\theta}))^{\top}
which is
(n-q)\times n
.
Define also
\bm{\Lambda}_{n}(\bm{\theta})
as the
q\times q
diagonal matrix containing on the diagonal the largest
q
eigenvalues
\lambda^{x}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})
,
j=1,\ldots,q
and denote by
\bm{\Phi}_{n}(\bm{\theta})
the
(n-q)\times(n-q)
diagonal matrix containing on the diagonal the remaining eigenvalues
\lambda^{x}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})
,
j=q+1,\ldots,n
. The spectral decomposition yields

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}_{n}^{x}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bm{P}}^{\dagger}_{n}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Lambda}_{n}(\bm{\theta})\bm{P}_{n}(\bm{\theta})+{\bm{Q}}^{\dagger}_{n}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Phi}_{n}(\bm{\theta})\bm{Q}_{n}(\bm{\theta}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bm{P}}^{\dagger}_{n}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{P}_{n}}(\bm{\theta})+{\bm{Q}}^{\dagger}_{n}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{Q}}_{n}(\bm{\theta}),

(B.4)

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bm{P}_{n}}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{P}^{\dagger}_{n}}(\bm{\theta}).

Now, let
\underline{\bm{P}}_{n}(L)

and

\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{-1/2}_{n}(L)

be the spatio-temporal linear filters built as in (
7
) from
\bm{P}_{n}(\bm{\theta})
and
\bm{\Lambda}^{-1/2}_{n}(\bm{\theta})
, respectively. Notice that since
\bm{\Sigma}_{n}^{x}(\bm{\theta})
is positive definite by construction, then
\bm{\Lambda}_{n}(\bm{\theta})
is bounded away from zero for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
and also bounded for any fixed
n
. So, recall the notation in (
8
),

{\bm{\psi}}^{n}_{\bm{\varsigma}}=\left(\psi^{n}_{1\bm{\varsigma}}\cdots\psi^{n}_{q\bm{\varsigma}}\right)^{\top}=\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{-1/2}_{n}(L)\star\underline{\bm{P}}_{n}(L)\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}

(B.5)

is well defined and it is a
q
-dimensional orthonormal white noise rf since by (
B.4
) its spectral density is (see Remark
A.2
):
\bm{\Sigma}^{\bm{\psi}^{n}}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbf{I}_{q}
.

For
\bm{M}\subset\bm{\Theta}
, let
\bm{K}_{M}\subset L^{q\times q}_{\infty}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C})
whose elements
\bm{C}
are
q\times q
complex matrices with elements being functions defined on
\bm{\Theta}
and such that: (a)
\bm{C}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{0}
for
\bm{\theta}\notin\bm{M}
, (b)
\bm{C}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{C}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbf{I}_{q}
for
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{M}
.
For any
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\bm{C}\in\bm{K}_{M}
, so that
\underline{\bm{C}}(L){\bm{\psi}}^{m}_{\bm{\varsigma}}\in\bm{\mathcal{X}}
and it is still a
q
-dimensional orthonormal white noise rf. Let us consider the orthogonal projection of this new white noise on the space
\overline{\text{span}}(\{\psi^{n}_{j\bm{\varsigma}},j=1,\ldots,q,\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\})
.
To this end, note that (
B.4
) yields
\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}=\underline{{\bm{P}}}^{\dagger}_{n}(L)\star\underline{{\bm{P}}}_{n}(L)\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}+\underline{{\bm{Q}}}^{\dagger}_{n}(L)\star\underline{{\bm{Q}}}_{n}(L)\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}
, thus

\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}=\underline{{\bm{P}}}^{\dagger}_{n}(L)\star\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{1/2}_{n}(L){\bm{\psi}}^{n}_{\bm{\varsigma}}+\underline{{\bm{Q}}}^{\dagger}_{n}(L)\star\underline{{\bm{Q}}}_{n}(L)\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}},

(B.6)

where
{{\bm{P}}}^{\dagger}_{n}{\bm{\Lambda}}^{1/2}_{n}\in L^{n\times q}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C})
due to integrability of the dynamic spatio-temporal eigenvalues (see Remark
3.1
).
Moreover, since
\bm{Q}_{n}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Sigma}_{n}^{x}(\bm{\theta}){{\bm{P}}}^{\dagger}_{n}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{\Phi}_{n}(\bm{\theta})\bm{Q}_{n}(\bm{\theta}){{\bm{P}}}^{\dagger}_{n}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{0}
for all
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
, the two terms on the right-hand side of (
B.6
) are orthogonal at any lead and lag element by element. Therefore, the first term is the projection of
\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}
on
\overline{\text{span}}(\{\psi^{n}_{j\bm{\varsigma}},j=1,\ldots,q,\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\})
and the second term is the residual. By applying
\underline{\bm{C}}(L)\star\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{-1/2}_{m}(L)\star\underline{\bm{P}}_{m}(L)
to both sides of (
B.6
) and using that
\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{-1/2}_{m}(L)\star\underline{\bm{P}}_{m}(L)\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}=\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{-1/2}_{m}(L)\star\underline{\bm{P}}_{m}(L)\bm{x}_{m\bm{\varsigma}}={\bm{\psi}}^{m}_{\bm{\varsigma}}
, we obtain the projection as follows

\underline{\bm{C}}(L){\bm{\psi}}^{m}_{\bm{\varsigma}}=\underline{\bm{D}}(L){\bm{\psi}}^{n}_{\bm{\varsigma}}+\underline{\bm{R}}(L)\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}},

(B.7)

where

{\bm{D}}(\bm{\theta})={\bm{C}}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{\Lambda}}^{-1/2}_{m}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{P}}_{m}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{P}}^{\dagger}_{n}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{\Lambda}}^{1/2}_{n}(\bm{\theta}),\hskip 9.24994pt{\bm{R}}(\bm{\theta})={\bm{C}}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Lambda}_{m}^{-1/2}(\bm{\theta})\bm{P}_{m}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{Q}}^{\dagger}_{n}(\bm{\theta})\bm{Q}_{n}(\bm{\theta}).

(B.8)

Notice that by taking the spectral density matrices of both sides of (
B.7
) yields

\mathbf{I}_{q}=\bm{D}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{D}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})+\bm{R}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Sigma}_{n}^{x}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{R}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta}).

(B.9)

Now, let us denote by
\mu(\bm{\theta})
the largest eigenvalue of the spectral density matrix of
\underline{\bm{R}}(L)\bm{x}_{n\bm{s}t}
. Then, we have

Lemma B.1
.

Suppose that (i) and (ii) of Theorem
4.1
, and Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
hold. Then, for
m<n
and
\bm{C}\in\bm{K}_{M}
,

\mu(\bm{\theta})\leq\lambda_{n,q+1}^{x}(\bm{\theta})/\lambda_{mq}^{x}(\bm{\theta}).

Proof.

Due to (
B.4
) both
\mathbf{I}_{n}-{\bm{Q}}^{\dagger}_{n}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{Q}}_{n}(\bm{\theta})
and
\lambda_{n,q+1}^{x}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{Q}}^{\dagger}_{n}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{Q}}_{n}(\bm{\theta})-{\bm{Q}}^{\dagger}_{n}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Phi}_{n}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{Q}}_{n}(\bm{\theta})
are non-negative definite. Therefore,
\lambda_{n,q+1}^{x}(\bm{\theta})\mathbf{I}_{n}-{\bm{Q}}^{\dagger}_{n}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Phi}_{n}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{Q}}_{n}(\bm{\theta})
is non-negative definite too, which implies that

\bm{C}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{\Lambda}}^{-1/2}_{m}(\bm{\theta})\bm{P}_{m}(\bm{\theta})(\lambda_{n,q+1}^{x}(\bm{\theta})\mathbf{I}_{n}-{\bm{Q}}^{\dagger}_{n}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Phi}_{n}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{Q}}_{n}(\bm{\theta})){\bm{P}}^{\dagger}_{m}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{\Lambda}}^{-1/2}_{m}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{C}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})

is also non-negative definite. Moreover,

\displaystyle\bm{C}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{\Lambda}}^{-1/2}_{m}(\bm{\theta})\bm{P}_{m}(\bm{\theta})(\lambda_{n,q+1}^{x}(\bm{\theta})\mathbf{I}_{n}

\displaystyle-{\bm{Q}}^{\dagger}_{n}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Phi}_{n}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{Q}}_{n}(\bm{\theta})){\bm{P}}^{\dagger}_{m}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{\Lambda}}^{-1/2}_{m}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{C}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{n,q+1}^{x}(\bm{\theta})\bm{C}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{\Lambda}}^{-1}_{m}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{C}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})-\bm{R}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Sigma}_{n}^{x}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{R}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta}),

(B.10)

where we obtain the second term making use of
\bm{\Phi}_{n}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{Q}_{n}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Sigma}_{n}^{x}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{Q}}^{\dagger}_{n}(\bm{\theta})
.
Letting
\bm{A}(\bm{\theta})=\lambda_{n,q+1}^{x}(\bm{\theta})\bm{C}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{\Lambda}}^{-1}_{m}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{C}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})
and
\bm{B}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{R}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Sigma}_{n}^{x}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{R}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})
we have that

\nu_{\max}\left(\bm{A}(\bm{\theta})\right)-\nu_{\max}(\bm{B}(\bm{\theta}))\geq\nu_{\min}\left(\bm{A}(\bm{\theta})\right)-\nu_{\max}(\bm{B}(\bm{\theta}))\geq\nu_{\min}\left(\bm{A}(\bm{\theta})-\bm{B}(\bm{\theta})\right)\geq 0,

where the left inequality follows from Weyl’s inequality; see Appendix
B.1
. Then,

0\leq\nu_{\max}\left(\bm{A}(\bm{\theta})\right)-\nu_{\max}(\bm{B}(\bm{\theta}))=\lambda_{n,q+1}^{x}(\bm{\theta})\nu_{\max}\left(\bm{C}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{\Lambda}}^{-1}_{m}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{C}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})\right)-\mu(\bm{\theta}).

Using
\nu_{\max}\left(\bm{C}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{\Lambda}}^{-1}_{m}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{C}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})\right)=1/\nu_{\min}({\bm{\Lambda}}_{m}(\bm{\theta}))=1/\lambda_{mq}^{x}(\bm{\theta})
concludes the proof.
∎

With the projection as in (
B.7
), we are now ready to construct our converging sequence. Under (i) and (ii) of Theorem
4.1
, there exists a set
\bm{\Pi}\subseteq\bm{\Theta}
and a real number
W
such that
\bm{\Theta}\setminus\bm{\Pi}
has null measure and (1)
\lambda_{nq+1}^{x}(\bm{\theta})\leq W
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and any
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Pi}
; (2)
\lim_{n\to\infty}\lambda_{nq}^{x}(\bm{\theta})=\infty
for any
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Pi}
. Let
\bm{M}
be a positive measure subset of
\bm{\Pi}
and
\{\alpha_{n},n\in\mathbb{N}\}
a real positive nondecreasing sequence such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\alpha_{n}=\infty
and
\lambda_{nq}^{x}(\bm{\theta})\geq\alpha_{n}
for
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{M}
.
Then, for
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{M}
according to Lemma
B.1
,
\mu(\bm{\theta})\leq\lambda_{n,q+1}^{x}(\bm{\theta})/\lambda_{mq}^{x}(\bm{\theta})\leq W/\alpha_{m}
. Denote by
\Delta_{j}(\bm{\theta}),j=1,\ldots,q
the eigenvalues of
\bm{D}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{D}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})
in descending order. By (
B.9
) and Weyl’s inequality, we have

1\geq\Delta_{q}(\bm{\theta})\geq 1-W/\alpha_{m}

(B.11)

for any
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{M}
. Hence, if
m^{*}
is such that
W/\alpha_{m^{*}}<1
, we have

\Delta_{q}(\bm{\theta})\geq 1-W/\alpha_{m^{*}}>0

(B.12)

for any
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{M}
and
m\geq m^{*}
.

Assuming
m\geq m^{*}
, we denote by
\bm{\Delta}(\bm{\theta})
the
q\times q
diagonal matrix with
\Delta_{j}(\bm{\theta})
,
j=1,\ldots,q
, on the diagonal. Let
\bm{H}(\bm{\theta})
be a matrix that is measurable in
\bm{M}
and satisfies that for any
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{M}
,
\bm{H}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{H}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{I}_{q}
and
\bm{H}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Delta}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{H}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{D}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{D}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})
. Notice that due to (
B.12
),
\Delta_{j}^{-1/2}(\bm{\theta})
is bounded for all
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{M}
and all
j=1,\ldots,q
. Therefore,

\bm{F}(\bm{\theta})=\begin{cases}\bm{H}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Delta}^{-1/2}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{H}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})\bm{D}(\bm{\theta})&\text{if $\bm{\theta}\in\bm{M}$}\\
\bm{0}&\text{if $\bm{\theta}\notin\bm{M}$}\end{cases}

(B.13)

is well defined and, clearly, it belongs to
\bm{K}_{M}
. We then have the following result:

Lemma B.2
.

Suppose that (i) and (ii) of Theorem
4.1
hold. Then given
\tau
such that
0<\tau<2
, there exists an integer
m_{\tau}
such that: (1)
W/\alpha_{m_{\tau}}<1
; (2) for
n>m>m_{\tau}
, the largest eigenvalue of the spectral density matrix of
\underline{\bm{C}}(L){\bm{\psi}}^{m}_{\bm{\varsigma}}-\underline{\bm{F}}(L){\bm{\psi}}^{n}_{\bm{\varsigma}}
is less than
\tau
for any
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Pi}
.

Proof.

Denote by
\bm{S}(\bm{\theta})
the spectral density matrix of
\underline{\bm{C}}(L){\bm{\psi}}^{m}_{\bm{\varsigma}}-\underline{\bm{F}}(L){\bm{\psi}}^{n}_{\bm{\varsigma}}
. Note that due to (
B.7
),

\underline{\bm{C}}(L){\bm{\psi}}^{m}_{\bm{\varsigma}}-\underline{\bm{F}}(L){\bm{\psi}}^{n}_{\bm{\varsigma}}=\underline{\bm{R}}(L)\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}+(\underline{\bm{D}}(L)-\underline{\bm{F}}(L)){\bm{\psi}}^{n}_{\bm{\varsigma}},

where two terms on the right-hand side are orthogonal at any lead and lag. Denoting by
\bm{S}_{1}(\bm{\theta})
and
\bm{S}_{2}(\bm{\theta})
the spectral density matrices of these terms respectively, then, for any
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{M}
, we have

\displaystyle\bm{S}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=\bm{S}_{1}(\bm{\theta})+\bm{S}_{2}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=\bm{R}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Sigma}_{n}^{x}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{R}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})+\bm{D}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{D}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})+\bm{F}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{F}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})-\bm{D}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{F}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})-\bm{F}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{D}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=2\mathbf{I}_{q}-\bm{D}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{F}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})-\bm{F}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{D}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=2\mathbf{I}_{q}-2\bm{H}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Delta}^{1/2}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{H}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=2\bm{H}(\bm{\theta})(\mathbf{I}_{q}-\bm{\Delta}^{1/2}(\bm{\theta})){\bm{H}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta}),

where the third equality is due to (
B.9
). Therefore, the largest eigenvalue of
\bm{S}(\bm{\theta})
is
2-2\sqrt{\Delta_{q}(\bm{\theta})}
, which is
(recall (
B.11
)) less than or equal to
2-2\Delta_{q}(\bm{\theta})\leq 2W/\alpha_{m}
. Since
\tau<2
, the result follows from taking a positive integer
m_{\tau}
such that

2W/\alpha_{m_{\tau}}<\tau.

(B.14)

This completes the proof.
∎

Denote by
\mathcal{S}(x_{i\bm{\varsigma}},x_{j\bm{\varsigma}};\bm{\theta})
the cross-spectrum between
x_{i\bm{\varsigma}},x_{j\bm{\varsigma}}
for
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
. The following intermediate lemmas will be used for proving further results.

Lemma B.3
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
hold. Consider the scalar sequences
\{A_{n\bm{\varsigma}},n\in\mathbb{N}\}
and
\{B_{n\bm{\varsigma}},n\in\mathbb{N}\}
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}A_{n\bm{\varsigma}}=A_{\bm{\varsigma}}
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}B_{n\bm{\varsigma}}=B_{\bm{\varsigma}}
, for any
\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, with
A_{\bm{\varsigma}},B_{\bm{\varsigma}}\in\bm{\mathcal{X}}
. Suppose also that the rf
A_{n},B_{n},A,
and
B
are co-homostationary with
x_{\ell}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, there exists a sequence
\{s_{i},i\in\mathbb{N},\,s_{i}<s_{i+1}\}
, such that
\{A_{s_{i}\bm{\varsigma}},i\in\mathbb{N}\}
and
\{B_{s_{i}\bm{\varsigma}},i\in\mathbb{N}\}
satisfy

\underset{i\to\infty}{\lim}\ \mathcal{S}(A_{s_{i}\bm{\varsigma}},B_{s_{i}\bm{\varsigma}};\bm{\theta})=\mathcal{S}(A_{\bm{\varsigma}},B_{\bm{\varsigma}};\bm{\theta})
,
\mathcal{L}
-a.e. in
\bm{\Theta}
.

Proof.

First, we have
\langle A_{n\bm{\varsigma}},B_{n\bm{\varsigma}}\rangle=(8\pi^{3})^{-1}\int_{\bm{\Theta}}\mathcal{S}(A_{n\bm{\varsigma}},B_{n\bm{\varsigma}};\bm{\theta}){\mathrm{d}}\bm{\theta}
. Then, due to continuity of the inner product and convergence of
A_{n\bm{\varsigma}}
and
B_{n\bm{\varsigma}}
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{8\pi^{3}}\int_{\bm{\Theta}}|\mathcal{S}(A_{n\bm{\varsigma}},B_{n\bm{\varsigma}};\bm{\theta})-\mathcal{S}(A_{\bm{\varsigma}},B_{\bm{\varsigma}};\bm{\theta})|{\mathrm{d}}\bm{\theta}=0.

The desired result follows from

Royden and Fitzpatrick 1988
.
∎

Lemma B.4
.

Suppose that (i) and (ii) of Theorem
4.1
and Assumptions
3.1
-
3.3
hold. Then there exists a
q
-dimensional orthonormal rf
\bm{v}
such that

(a)

v_{j\bm{\varsigma}}\in\mathcal{G}(\bm{x})
for all
j=1,\ldots,q
;

(b)

the spectral density matrix of
\bm{v}
is
\mathbf{I}_{q}

\mathcal{L}
-a.e. in
\bm{M}
, whilst it is
{\mathbf{0}}
for
\bm{\theta}\notin\bm{M}
.

Proof.

The proof goes through by repeatedly applying Lemma
B.2
to construct a Cauchy sequence whose limit satisfies the desired properties.

Let
\bm{F}_{1}(\bm{\theta})
be an element of
\bm{K}_{M}
. Let also
\tau=1/2^{2}
,
n_{1}=m_{\tau}
, where
m_{\tau}
satisfies (
B.14
),
\bm{G}_{1}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{F}_{1}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Lambda}_{n_{1}}^{-1/2}(\bm{\theta})\bm{P}_{n_{1}}(\bm{\theta})
, and
\bm{v}_{\bm{\varsigma}}^{(1)}=\underline{\bm{G}}_{1}(L)\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}
. One can easily check that the spectral density matrix of
\bm{v}^{(1)}
equals
\mathbf{I}_{q}
for
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{M}
,
{\mathbf{0}}_{q}
for
\bm{\theta}\notin\bm{M}
.

In the similar way, set
\tau=1/2^{4}
and
n_{2}=m_{\tau}
, where
m_{\tau}
satisfies (
B.14
) and
m_{\tau}\geq n_{1}
. Set
\bm{D}(\bm{\theta})
in (
B.8
) by replacing
\bm{C}(\bm{\theta})
,
n
,
m
with
\bm{F}_{1}(\bm{\theta})
,
n_{2}
,
n_{1}
, respectively, and set
\bm{F}_{2}(\bm{\theta})
as in (
B.13
). Then set
\bm{G}_{2}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{F}_{2}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Lambda}_{n_{2}}^{-1/2}(\bm{\theta})\bm{P}_{n_{2}}(\bm{\theta})
and
\bm{v}_{\bm{\varsigma}}^{(2)}=\underline{\bm{G}}_{2}(L)\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}
. The spectral density matrix of
\bm{v}^{(2)}

equals
\mathbf{I}_{q}
for
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{M}
,
{\mathbf{0}}_{q}
for
\bm{\theta}\notin\bm{M}
. Denote by
\mathcal{A}_{1}(\bm{\theta})
the largest eigenvalue of the spectral matrix of
\bm{v}^{(1)}-\bm{v}^{(2)}
. According to the definition of
n_{1}
and Lemma
B.2
,
\mathcal{A}_{1}(\bm{\theta})<1/2^{2}
for any
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Pi}
, which entails
\|v_{j\bm{\varsigma}}^{(1)}-v_{j\bm{\varsigma}}^{(2)}\|<1/2
for all
j=1,\ldots,q
and any
\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
.

By recursion, set
\tau=1/2^{2k}
and
n_{k}=m_{\tau}
, where
m_{\tau}
satisfies (
B.14
) and
m_{\tau}\geq n_{k-1}
. Set
\bm{D}(\bm{\theta})
in (
B.8
) by replacing
\bm{C}(\bm{\theta})
,
n
,
m
with
\bm{F}_{k-1}(\bm{\theta})
,
n_{k}
,
n_{k-1}
, respectively, and set
\bm{F}_{k}(\bm{\theta})
as in (
B.13
). Then set
\bm{G}_{k}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{F}_{k}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Lambda}_{n_{k}}^{-1/2}(\bm{\theta})\bm{P}_{n_{k}}(\bm{\theta})
and
\bm{v}_{\bm{\varsigma}}^{(k)}=\underline{\bm{G}}_{k}(L)\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}
. The spectral density matrix of
\bm{v}^{(k)}
equals
\mathbf{I}_{q}
for
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{M}
,
{\mathbf{0}}_{q}
for
\bm{\theta}\notin\bm{M}
. Denote by
\mathcal{A}_{k-1}(\bm{\theta})
the largest eigenvalue of the spectral matrix of
\bm{v}^{(k-1)}-\bm{v}^{(k)}
. According to the definition of
n_{k-1}
and Lemma
B.2
,
\mathcal{A}_{k-1}(\bm{\theta})<1/2^{2(k-1)}
for any
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Pi}
, which entails
\|v_{j\bm{\varsigma}}^{(k-1)}-v_{j\bm{\varsigma}}^{(k)}\|<1/2^{k-1}
for all
j=1,\ldots,q
and any
\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
.

Hence, for all
j=1,\ldots,q
and any
\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, we have

\|v_{j\bm{\varsigma}}^{(k)}-v_{j\bm{\varsigma}}^{(k+h)}\|\leq\|v_{j\bm{\varsigma}}^{(k)}-v_{j\bm{\varsigma}}^{(k+1)}\|+\cdots+\|v_{j\bm{\varsigma}}^{(k+h-1)}-v_{j\bm{\varsigma}}^{(k+h)}\|<\sum_{j=k}^{k+h-1}\frac{1}{2^{j}}<\frac{1}{2^{k-1}},

which implies that for all
j=1,\ldots,q
,
\{{v}_{j\bm{\varsigma}}^{(k)},k\in\mathbb{N}\}
is a Cauchy sequence. Denote by
\bm{v}_{\bm{\varsigma}}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\bm{v}_{\bm{\varsigma}}^{(k)}
. Then (b) follows from Lemma
B.3
and the fact that the spectral density matrix of
\bm{v}^{(k)}
equals
\mathbf{I}_{q}
for
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{M}
,
{\mathbf{0}}_{q}
for
\bm{\theta}\notin\bm{M}
.

Now it remains to prove (a), for which it suffices to show that each row of
\{\bm{G}_{k},k\in\mathbb{N}\}
is a STDAS (see Definition
4.1
). Notice that
\bm{G}_{k}(\bm{\theta})\bm{G}^{\dagger}_{k}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{F}_{k}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Lambda}_{n_{k}}^{-1}(\bm{\theta})\bm{F}^{\dagger}_{k}(\bm{\theta})
, whose diagonal entries are equal or less than
1/\lambda_{n_{k}q}^{x}(\bm{\theta})
since
\bm{F}_{k}(\bm{\theta})\in\bm{K}_{M}
because
1/\lambda_{n_{k}q}^{x}(\bm{\theta})
converges to zero
\mathcal{L}\text{-a.e.}
in
\bm{\Theta}
by (ii) of Theorem
4.1
. Moreover, without loss of generality we can always restrict Assumption
3.3
to assume
\lambda_{n_{k}q}^{x}(\bm{\theta})>1
for all
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
(see the arguments in
Forni and Lippi 2001
, Section 4.2
). Then,
1/\lambda_{n_{k}q}^{x}(\bm{\theta})<1
and by Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem, its integral over
\bm{\Theta}
converges to zero. This concludes the proof of (a).
∎

Now, we apply the results in Lemma
B.4
to define a
q
-dimensional white noise rf over all
\bm{\Theta}
.

Proposition B.5
.

Suppose that (i) and (ii) of Theorem
4.1
and Assumptions
3.1
-
3.3
hold. There exists a
q
-dimensional orthonormal white noise rf
\bm{z}

such that, for all
j=1,\ldots,q
,
z_{j\bm{\varsigma}}\in\mathcal{G}(\bm{x})
.

Proof.

The proof goes through by choosing a set
\bm{N}\in\bm{\Theta}
with Lebesgue measure
\mathcal{L}(\bm{N})=8\pi^{3}=\mathcal{L}(\bm{\Theta})
and by obtaining a sequence of
q
-dimensional vector rf, which satisfy (a) in Lemma
B.4
and have spectral density matrix equal to
\mathbf{I}_{q}
for
\mathcal{L}\text{-a.e.}
in a partition of
\bm{N}
, and is
{\mathbf{0}}
otherwise.

Now, define
\bm{M}_{0}^{(1)}=\bm{\Pi}
. Then, by recursion, define
\nu_{a}
,
a\in\mathbb{N}
, as the smallest among the integer
m
such that

\mathcal{L}(\{\bm{\theta}\in\bm{M}_{a-1}^{(1)},\lambda_{mq}^{x}(\bm{\theta})>a\})>4\pi^{3}

and define

\bm{M}_{a}^{(1)}=\{\bm{\theta}\in\bm{M}_{a-1}^{(1)},\lambda_{\nu_{a}q}^{x}(\bm{\theta})>a\}.

Clearly, the Lebesgue measure of the set

\bm{N}_{1}=\bm{M}_{1}^{(1)}\cap\bm{M}_{2}^{(1)}\cap\cdots\cap\bm{M}_{a}^{(1)}\cap\cdots

is not less than
4\pi^{3}
. In the similar fashion, define
\bm{N}_{2}
starting with
\bm{M}_{0}^{(2)}=\bm{\Pi}\setminus\bm{N}_{1}
instead of
\bm{\Pi}
, and using
\mathcal{L}(\bm{\Pi}\setminus\bm{N}_{1})/2
instead of
4\pi^{3}
. Also, for
b>2
, define
\bm{N}_{b}
starting with
\bm{M}_{0}^{(b)}=\bm{\Pi}\setminus\bm{N}_{1}\setminus\bm{N}_{2}\setminus\cdots\setminus\bm{N}_{b-1}
, and using
\mathcal{L}(\bm{\Pi}\setminus\bm{N}_{1}\setminus\bm{N}_{2}\setminus\cdots\setminus\bm{N}_{b-1})/2
. Letting
\bm{N}=\bm{N}_{1}\cup\bm{N}_{2}\cup\cdots
, we have

\mathcal{L}(\bm{N})=\mathcal{L}(\bm{N}_{1})+\mathcal{L}(\bm{N}_{2})+\cdots+\mathcal{L}(\bm{N}_{b})+\cdots=8\pi^{3},

since by construction
\bm{N}_{i}\cap\bm{N}_{j}=\emptyset
, for
i\neq j
and
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma
B.4
can be applied to the subset
\bm{N}_{b}
, with the sequence
\alpha_{n}
defined as
\alpha_{n}=a
, where
a
is the only integer such that
\nu_{a}\leq n<\nu_{a+1}
. Hence, we obtain a
q
-dimensional vector rf
\{\bm{v}_{\bm{\varsigma}}^{b}=(v_{1\bm{\varsigma}}^{b}\ v_{2\bm{\varsigma}}^{b}\ \cdots\ v_{q\bm{\varsigma}}^{b})^{\top},\,\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\}
such that (i)
v_{j\bm{\varsigma}}^{b}\in\mathcal{G}(\bm{x})
for all
j=1,\ldots,q
; (ii) its spectral density matrix equals
\mathbf{I}_{q}
for
\mathcal{L}\text{-a.e.}
in
\bm{N}_{b}
, and is
{\mathbf{0}}_{q}
for
\bm{\theta}\notin\bm{N}_{b}
.

Finally, set
\bm{z}_{\bm{\varsigma}}=\sum_{b=1}^{\infty}\bm{v}_{\bm{\varsigma}}^{b}
. It is easy to see that
z_{j\bm{\varsigma}}\in\mathcal{G}(\bm{x})
for all
j=1,\ldots,q
and the spectral density matrix of
\bm{z}
equals
\mathbf{I}_{q}

\mathcal{L}\text{-a.e.}
in
\bm{\Theta}
. Therefore,
\bm{z}
is a
q
-dimensional orthonormal white noise rf.
∎

Considering the
q
-dimensional orthonormal white noise rf
\bm{z}
in Proposition
B.5
, we have the following

Proposition B.6
.

Suppose that (i) and (ii) of Theorem
4.1
and Assumptions
3.1
-
3.3
hold. Then
\text{\rm$\overline{\text{span}}$}(\bm{z})=\mathcal{G}(\bm{x})
.

Proof.

Consider a scalar rf
y_{\bm{\varsigma}}\in\mathcal{G}(\bm{x})
and consider the projection

y_{\bm{\varsigma}}=\text{proj}(y_{\bm{\varsigma}}|\overline{\text{span}}(\bm{z}))+r_{\bm{\varsigma}}.

It suffices to prove that
r_{\bm{\varsigma}}=0
. Let
\bm{W}(\bm{\theta})
denote the spectral density matrix of the
(q+1)
-dimensional rf
\{(\bm{z}_{\bm{\varsigma}}\ r_{\bm{\varsigma}})^{\top},\,\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\}
. According to the proof of Proposition
B.5
,
\bm{W}(\bm{\theta})
is diagonal with
\mathbf{I}_{q}
in the
q\times q
upper-left submatrix and
\text{det}(\bm{W}(\bm{\theta}))=\mathcal{S}(r_{\bm{\varsigma}},r_{\bm{\varsigma}};\bm{\theta}).
Since both
\bm{z}
and
r
belong to
\mathcal{G}(\bm{x})
, there exist STDASs
\{\bm{a}_{nj},n\in\mathbb{N}\}
, for
j=1,\ldots,q+1
such that,

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\lim}\ \bm{a}_{nj}(L)\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}=z_{j\bm{\varsigma}},\hskip 9.24994ptj=1,\ldots,q,

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\lim}\ \bm{a}_{n,q+1}(L)\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}=r_{\bm{\varsigma}}.

Now, for all
j=1,\ldots,q+1
, by Definition
4.1
of STDAS, we must have
\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\bm{\Theta}}\bm{a}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})\bm{a}_{nj}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta}){\mathrm{d}}\bm{\theta}=0
, it follows that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\bm{a}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})\bm{a}_{nj}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})=\lim_{n\to\infty}|\bm{a}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})|^{2}=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{L}\;\text{a.e. in}\;\bm{\Theta}.

(B.15)

Therefore (see
Royden and Fitzpatrick 1988
)
there exists a sub-sequence
\{s_{k},\,k\in\mathbb{N},s_{k}<s_{k+1}\}
, defining a corresponding sub-set of
s_{k}
elements of
\bm{a}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})
collected into the
s_{k}
-dimensional row vector
\bm{a}_{s_{k}j}(\bm{\theta})
, which is such that

\lim_{k\to\infty}|\bm{a}_{s_{k}j}(\bm{\theta})|^{2}=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{L}\;\text{a.e. in}\;\bm{\Theta}.

(B.16)

Define also the rf
\bm{x}_{s_{k}\bm{\varsigma}}
obtained from
\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}
by setting to zero all entries with the exception of the
s_{k}
elements corresponding to the sub-sequence
\{s_{k}\}
, and
let
\bm{Z}_{n}(\bm{\theta})
denote the spectral density matrix of
\{(\bm{a}_{n1}(L)\bm{x}_{s_{k}\bm{\varsigma}}\cdots\bm{a}_{n,q+1}(L)\bm{x}_{s_{k}\bm{\varsigma}})^{\top},\,\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\}
.
In view of Lemma
B.3
, there exists a sub-sequence of
\bm{Z}_{n}(\bm{\theta})
converging to
\bm{W}(\bm{\theta})
,
\mathcal{L}
a.e. in
\bm{\Theta}
. Therefore, without loss of generality, we can assume that
\bm{Z}_{n}(\bm{\theta})
converges to
\bm{W}(\bm{\theta})
,
\mathcal{L}
a.e. in
\bm{\Theta}
.

Let
\bm{f}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{a}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})\bm{P}_{s_{k}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})
and
\bm{g}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{a}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})-\bm{f}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})\bm{P}_{s_{k}}(\bm{\theta})
for
j=1,\ldots,q+1
and
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
. Hence,
\bm{a}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{f}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})\bm{P}_{s_{k}}(\bm{\theta})+\bm{g}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})
and, for all
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
,

|\bm{a}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})|^{2}=|\bm{f}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})|^{2}+|\bm{g}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})|^{2}.

(B.17)

Indeed, by definition,

\bm{f}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})\bm{P}_{s_{k}}(\bm{\theta})\bm{g}_{nj}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{a}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})\bm{P}_{s_{k}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})\bm{P}_{s_{k}}(\bm{\theta})\left(\bm{a}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})-\bm{a}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})\bm{P}_{s_{k}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})\bm{P}_{s_{k}}(\bm{\theta})\right)^{\dagger}=0.

Now, (
B.15
) implies

\lim_{n\to\infty}|\bm{g}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})|^{2}=0\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{L}\;\text{a.e. in}\;\bm{\Theta},

(B.18)

and we have also that

\lim_{n\to\infty}|\bm{f}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})|^{2}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\bm{a}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})\bm{P}_{s_{k}}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})\bm{P}_{s_{k}}(\bm{\theta})\bm{a}^{\dagger}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})=\lim_{k\to\infty}|\bm{a}_{s_{k}j}(\bm{\theta})|^{2}=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{L}\;\text{a.e. in}\;\bm{\Theta}.

(B.19)

It follows that the following orthogonal decomposition holds:

\underline{\bm{a}}_{nj}(L)\bm{x}_{s_{k}\bm{\varsigma}}=\underline{\bm{f}}_{nj}(L)\star\underline{\bm{P}}_{s_{k}}(L)\bm{x}_{s_{k}\bm{\varsigma}}+\underline{\bm{g}}_{nj}(L)\bm{x}_{s_{k}\bm{\varsigma}},\hskip 9.24994ptj=1,\ldots,q+1.

(B.20)

Denote by
\bm{Z}_{n}^{1}(\bm{\theta})
and
\bm{Z}_{n}^{2}(\bm{\theta})
the spectral density matrices of the rf

\left\{\left(\underline{\bm{f}}_{n1}(L)\star\underline{\bm{P}}_{s_{k}}(L)\bm{x}_{s_{k}\bm{\varsigma}}\cdots\underline{\bm{f}}_{n,q+1}(L)\star\underline{\bm{P}}_{s_{k}}(L)\bm{x}_{s_{k}\bm{\varsigma}}\right)^{\dagger},\,\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\right\}

and

\left\{\left((\underline{\bm{g}}_{n1}(L)\bm{x}_{s_{k}\bm{\varsigma}}\cdots\underline{\bm{g}}_{n,q+1}(L)\bm{x}_{s_{k}\bm{\varsigma}}\right)^{\top},\,\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\right\},

respectively. Because of (
B.17
) and (
B.20
), we then have

\bm{Z}_{n}(\bm{\theta})=\bm{Z}_{n}^{1}(\bm{\theta})+\bm{Z}_{n}^{2}(\bm{\theta}).

Notice that
\bm{Z}_{n}^{1}(\bm{\theta})
is singular for all
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
, as
k\to\infty
, because
\bm{P}_{s_{k}}(\bm{\theta})
is
q+1\times s_{k}
. Hence,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\text{det}(\bm{Z}_{n}^{1}(\bm{\theta}))=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}.

(B.21)

Since
\bm{g}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})
is orthogonal to
{\bm{p}}_{s_{k}i}^{x}(\bm{\theta})
for
i=1,\ldots,q
, we have

\bm{Z}_{n}^{2}(\bm{\theta}))=\bm{g}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Sigma}_{s_{k}}^{x}(\bm{\theta})\bm{g}_{nj}^{\dagger}(\bm{\theta})\leq\lambda_{s_{k}q+1}^{x}(\bm{\theta})|\bm{g}_{nj}(\bm{\theta})|^{2}

(
Lancaster and Tismenetsky 1985
, Exercise 1, p. 287)
. Now, because of (i) in Theorem
4.1
and by (
B.18
) we have that
\bm{Z}_{n}^{2}(\bm{\theta})
converges to zero
\mathcal{L}\text{-a.e.}
in
\bm{\Theta}
as
n\to\infty
.
Therefore, by (
B.21
),

\lim_{n\to\infty}\text{det}(\bm{Z}_{n}(\bm{\theta}))=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{L}\text{ a.e. in }\bm{\Theta},

(B.22)

which entails that
\text{det}(\bm{W}(\bm{\theta}))=\mathcal{S}(r_{\bm{\varsigma}},r_{\bm{\varsigma}};\bm{\theta})=0
,
\mathcal{L}\text{-a.e.}
in
\bm{\Theta}
and, thus,
r_{\bm{\varsigma}}=0
.
∎

B.2.2
Canonical decomposition into common and idiosyncratic component

Consider the canonical decomposition

\displaystyle x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}

\displaystyle=\text{proj}(x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}|\mathcal{G}(\bm{x}))+\delta_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}=\gamma_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}+\delta_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}},\;\text{ say.}

(B.23)

So far by means of Propositions
B.5
and
B.6
, we have shown that if (i) and (ii) of Theorem
4.1
hold, then, there exists a
q
-dimensional orthonormal white noise rf
\bm{z}
such that

\displaystyle\gamma_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}

\displaystyle=\underline{\bm{c}}_{\ell}(L)\bm{z}_{\bm{\varsigma}},

(B.24)

with
{\bm{c}}_{\ell}\in L_{2}^{q}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C}).

Now, consider a generic
n
-dimensional rf
\bm{w}_{n}
satisfying Assumptions
3.1
-
3.2
with dynamic spatio-temporal eigenvalues
\lambda_{nj}^{w}(\bm{\theta})
,
j=1,\ldots,n
,
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
.
Then, for any
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
let
\lambda_{j}^{w}(\bm{\theta})=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{nj}^{w}(\bm{\theta})
and recall that, since
\lambda_{nj}^{w}(\bm{\theta})
is an increasing sequence in
n
then
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{nj}^{w}(\bm{\theta})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\lambda_{nj}^{w}(\bm{\theta})
. So
\lambda_{j}^{w}(\bm{\theta})
is the
j
-th largest dynamic spatio-temporal eigenvalue of the infinite dimensional spectral density matrix
\bm{\Sigma}^{w}(\bm{\theta})
of the infinite dimensional rf
\bm{w}
.
The proof of the sufficient condition is concluded by means of the next two results.

Proposition B.7
.

Under Assumptions
3.1
-
3.2
, the following statements are equivalent:

(a)

\bm{w}=\{(w_{1\bm{\varsigma}}\ w_{2\bm{\varsigma}}\cdots w_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}\cdots)^{\top},\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\}
is idiosyncratic;

(b)

the function
\lambda^{w}_{1}:\bm{\Theta}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
is essentially bounded, i.e.,
\text{ess}\sup(\lambda_{1}^{w})<\infty
where
\text{ess}\sup(\lambda_{1}^{w})=\inf\{M:\mathcal{L}[\bm{\theta}:\lambda_{1}^{w}(\bm{\theta})>M]=0\}
;

(c)

Define
\bm{\Upsilon}:\bm{\Psi}\to L^{\infty}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C},\bm{\Sigma}^{w}_{n})
as
\bm{\Upsilon}(\bm{f})=\bm{f}
, the mapping
\bm{\Upsilon}
is continuous.

Proof.

We first show that (a) and (c) are equivalent,
(a)\Leftrightarrow(c)
. To this end, notice that

\|\underline{\bm{a}}_{n}(L)\bm{w}_{\bm{s}t}\|=\|\bm{a}_{n}\|_{\bm{\Sigma}^{w}}=\|\bm{\Upsilon}({\bm{a}}_{n})\|_{\bm{\Sigma}^{w}},

(B.25)

where the first equality follows from the application of the isometric isomorphism
\mathcal{J}^{-1}
(see Remarks
A.1
and
A.2
), while the second from the definition of the mapping
\bm{\Upsilon}
. Then, by definition of idiosyncratic process
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\underline{\bm{a}}_{n}(L)\bm{w}_{\bm{s}t}\|=0
, thus by (
B.25
) it follows that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\bm{\Upsilon}({\bm{a}}_{n})\|_{\bm{\Sigma}^{w}}=0
, which implies that the linear mapping
\bm{\Upsilon}
is continuous
at zero. From
Conway 1985
, it follows that
\bm{\Upsilon}
is continuous everywhere. This proves
(a)\Leftrightarrow(c)
.

To prove that (b) and (c) are equivalent,
(b)\Leftrightarrow(c)
, first notice that continuity and boundedness are equivalent for linear maps between normed vector spaces
(
Royden and Fitzpatrick 1988
, Theorem 1, p. 257)
. Then, consider the definition of operator norm:

\|\bm{\Upsilon}\|=\sup_{\bm{f}\in\bm{\Psi},\|\bm{f}\|=1}\left\|\bm{\Upsilon}(\bm{f})\right\|_{\bm{\Sigma}^{w}}

and notice that boundedness of
\bm{\Upsilon}
means that
\|\bm{\Upsilon}\|\leq c<\infty
, for
c\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. We now show that
\|\bm{\Upsilon}\|=\sqrt{\text{ess}\sup(\lambda_{1}^{w})}
.
This would imply that
(b)\Leftrightarrow(c)
.

Let us define,

\bm{f}^{[n]}
as the infinite dimensional vector with
f_{j}^{[n]}=f_{j}
for
j\leq n
and
f_{j}^{[n]}=0
for
j>n
,
\bm{f}^{\{n\}}
as the
n
-dimensional sub-vector made of the first
n
entries of
\bm{f}^{[n]}
,
and

\psi_{n}=\sup_{\bm{f}\in\bm{\Psi},\|\bm{f}\|=1}\left\|\bm{\Upsilon}(\bm{f}^{[n]})\right\|_{\bm{\Sigma}^{w}},

so, for
\bm{\Psi}_{n}=L^{\infty}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C})\cap L^{\infty}_{2}(\bm{\Theta},\mathbb{C},\lambda^{w}_{1n})
, we have

\displaystyle\psi^{2}_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{\bm{f}\in\bm{\Psi},\|\bm{f}\|=1}\frac{1}{8\pi^{3}}\int_{\bm{\Theta}}\bm{f}^{\{n\}}(\bm{\theta})\bm{\Sigma}^{w}_{n}(\bm{\theta}){\bm{f}}^{\{n\}{\dagger}}(\bm{\theta})\mathrm{d}\bm{\theta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{h\in\bm{\Psi}_{n},\|\bm{h}\|=1}\frac{1}{8\pi^{3}}\int_{\bm{\Theta}}\|h(\bm{\theta})\|^{2}\lambda^{w}_{1n}(\bm{\theta})\mathrm{d}\bm{\theta},

where the last equality follows form
Lancaster and Tismenetsky 1985
. Moreover,
Conway 1985
implies that
\psi_{n}^{2}=\text{ess}\sup\lambda_{1n}^{w}(\bm{\theta})
. Finally we notice that
\|\bm{\Upsilon}\|^{2}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi^{2}_{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\text{ess}\sup\lambda^{w}_{1n}(\bm{\theta})}

=\text{ess}\sup\lim_{n\to\infty}\lambda^{w}_{1n}(\bm{\theta})=\text{ess}\sup\lambda^{w}_{1}(\bm{\theta})
.
∎

Proposition B.8
.

Under Assumptions
3.1
-
3.2
,

\bm{\delta}=\{(\delta_{1\bm{\varsigma}}\ \delta_{2\bm{\varsigma}}\cdots\delta_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}\cdots)^{\top},\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\}
is idiosyncratic, where
\delta_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, are defined in (
B.23
).

Proof.

Start by considering again (
B.6
) and let,
\underline{\bm{\pi}}_{n\ell}(L)
and
\underline{\bm{q}}_{n\ell}(L)
be the
\ell
-th
q
-dimensional and
(n-q)
-dimensional rows of
\underline{{\bm{P}}}^{\dagger}_{n}(L)
and
\underline{{\bm{Q}}}^{\dagger}_{n}(L)
, respectively, for any given
\ell\leq n
. Then,

x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}=\underline{\bm{\pi}}_{n\ell}(L)\star\underline{{\bm{P}}}(L)\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}+\underline{\bm{q}}_{n\ell}(L)\star\underline{{\bm{Q}}}(L)\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}=\underline{\bm{\pi}}_{n\ell}(L)\star\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}_{n}^{1/2}(L)\bm{\psi}_{\bm{\varsigma}}^{n}+\underline{\bm{q}}_{n\ell}(L)\star\underline{{\bm{Q}}}(L)\bm{x}_{n\bm{\varsigma}}=\gamma_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}^{n}+\delta_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}^{n}.

(B.26)

For any given
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\bm{\Sigma}_{m}^{\delta}(\bm{\theta})
be the spectral density matrix of
\bm{\delta}_{m}=\{(\delta_{1\bm{\varsigma}}\cdots\delta_{m\bm{\varsigma}})^{\top},\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\}
and for
n>m

let
\bm{\Sigma}_{m}^{\delta^{n}}(\bm{\theta})
be the spectral density matrix of
\bm{\delta}_{m}^{n}=\{(\delta_{1\bm{\varsigma}}^{n}\cdots\delta_{m\bm{\varsigma}}^{n})^{\top},\bm{\varsigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\}
, where
\delta_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}^{n}=x_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}-\gamma_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}^{n}
(see (
B.26
)). Then, from Theorem
5.1
, we have that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\gamma_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}^{n}=\gamma_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}},

in mean-square, with
\gamma_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, defined in (
B.23
). Thus,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\delta_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}^{n}=\delta_{\ell\bm{\varsigma}}

in mean-square for any
\ell\leq m
. Notice that, although Theorem
5.1
is proved in the next section, its proof only requires (i) and (ii) in Theorem
4.1
to hold as in this proof, so there is no feedback loop between the two theorems.

By Lemma
B.3
, a sub-sequence of
\bm{\Sigma}_{m}^{\delta^{n}}(\bm{\theta})
converges to
\bm{\Sigma}_{m}^{\delta}(\bm{\theta})
,
\mathcal{L}\text{-a.e. in }\bm{\Theta}
.

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|\bm{\Sigma}_{m}^{\delta^{n}}(\bm{\theta})-\bm{\Sigma}_{m}^{\delta}(\bm{\theta})\right\|_{F}=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{L}\text{-a.e. in }\bm{\Theta}.

where
\|\cdot\|_{F}
denotes the Frobenius norm.
Then, by definition of the spectral norm, denoted as
\|\cdot\|
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left|\lambda_{m1}^{\delta^{n}}(\bm{\theta})-\lambda_{m1}^{\delta}(\bm{\theta})\right|=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|\bm{\Sigma}_{m}^{\delta^{n}}(\bm{\theta})-\bm{\Sigma}_{m}^{\delta}(\bm{\theta})\right\|\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|\bm{\Sigma}_{m}^{\delta^{n}}(\bm{\theta})-\bm{\Sigma}_{m}^{\delta}(\bm{\theta})\right\|_{F}=0.

(B.27)

Moreover, since
\bm{\Sigma}_{m}^{\delta^{n}}(\bm{\theta})
is the upper-left
m\times m
submatrix of
\bm{\Sigma}_{n}^{\delta^{n}}(\bm{\theta})
, we have by Weyl’s inequality and definition of
\delta_{n}^{n}
in (
B.26
),

\lambda_{m1}^{\delta^{n}}(\bm{\theta})\leq\lambda_{n1}^{\delta^{n}}(\bm{\theta})=\lambda_{n,q+1}^{x}(\bm{\theta}),

for any
m\leq n
, any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and any
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
. Therefore, letting
\lambda_{q+1}^{x}(\bm{\theta})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\lambda_{n,q+1}^{x}(\bm{\theta})
,
from (
B.27
)

\lambda_{m1}^{\delta}(\bm{\theta})\leq\lambda_{q+1}^{x}(\bm{\theta}),\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{L}\text{-a.e. in }\bm{\Theta},

and since this is true for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, then, letting
\lambda_{1}^{\delta}(\bm{\theta})=\lim_{m\to\infty}\lambda_{m1}^{\delta}(\bm{\theta})
,

\lambda_{1}^{\delta}(\bm{\theta})\leq\lambda_{q+1}^{x}(\bm{\theta}),\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{L}\text{-a.e. in }\bm{\Theta}.

(B.28)

So by (i) in Theorem
4.1
,
\lambda_{1}^{\delta}
is essentially bounded, and by Proposition
B.7
,
\bm{\delta}
is idiosyncratic.
∎

To conclude, by Weyl’s inequality and Propositions
B.7
and
B.8
, we have

\lambda_{nq}^{\gamma}(\bm{\theta})\geq\lambda_{nq}^{x}(\bm{\theta})-\lambda_{n1}^{\delta}(\bm{\theta})\geq\lambda_{nq}^{x}(\bm{\theta}),

for any given
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
. Therefore, given (ii) of Theorem
4.1
,
